Varifold solutions to volume-preserving mean curvature flow: existence and weak–strong uniqueness
Authors: Andrea Poiatti
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
In this contribution we introduce a novel weak solution concept for two-phase volume-preserving mean curvature flow, having both properties of unconditional global-in-time existence and weak–strong uniqueness. These solutions extend the ones proposed by Hensel and Laux (J Differ Geom 130:209–268, 2025) for the standard mean curvature flow, and consist in evolving varifolds coupled with the phase volumes by a transport equation. First, we show that, in the same setting as in Takasao (Arch Ration Mech Anal 247:52, 2023), any sharp interface limit of solutions to a slightly modified nonlocal Allen–Cahn equation is a varifold solution according to our new definition. Secondly, we crucially introduce a new notion of volume-preserving gradient-flow calibrations, allowing the extended velocity vector field to point in the normal direction on the interface. We show that any sufficiently regular strong solution is calibrated in this sense. Finally, we prove that any classical solution to volume-preserving mean curvature flow, which is then automatically a calibrated flow, is unique in the class of our new varifold solutions.

Introduction

Volume-preserving mean curvature flow is the most simple geometric evolution equation for closed hypersurfaces with the main property of preserving the enclosed volume. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d \ge 2$$\end{document}d≥2 be an integer and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω be either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd or the d-dimensional flat torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Td. Consider a family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}A(t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T)$$\end{document}t∈[0,T), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in (0,\infty ]$$\end{document}T∈(0,∞], of open sets with smooth boundary. The family of hypersurfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\partial \mathscr {A}(t)\}_{t\in [0,T)}$$\end{document}{∂A(t)}t∈[0,T) is a volume-preserving mean curvature flow if the normal velocity vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {V}} $$\end{document}V satisfies1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\textbf {V}} ={\textbf {H}} -\left( \frac{1}{\mathcal {H}^{d-1}(\partial \mathscr {A}(t))}\int _{\partial \mathscr {A}(t)}{\textbf {H}} \cdot {\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}\text {d} \mathcal {H}^{d-1}\right) {\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}V=H-1Hd-1(∂A(t))∫∂A(t)H·n∂AdHd-1n∂A,for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in (0, T)$$\end{document}t∈(0,T). Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Hd-1 is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(d - 1)$$\end{document}(d-1)-dimensional Hausdorff measure, whereas \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {H}} $$\end{document}H and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}$$\end{document}n∂A are the mean curvature vector and the inner unit normal vector of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial \mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}∂A(t), respectively. The fundamental property of this flow is that the enclosed volume is preserved, in the sense that1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\text {d} }{\text {d} t}\mathcal {L}^d(\mathscr {A}(t))=-\int _{\partial \mathscr {A}(t)}{\textbf {V}} \cdot {\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}\text {d} \mathcal {H}^{d-1}=0,\quad \forall t\in [0,T), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtLd(A(t))=-∫∂A(t)V·n∂AdHd-1=0,∀t∈[0,T),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^d$$\end{document}Ld is the d-dimensional Lebesgue measure. When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(0)$$\end{document}A(0) is convex, Gage [25] and Huisken [31] proved that there exists a solution to (1.1) converging to a sphere as time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}t→∞. Also, Escher and Simonett [18] showed the short time existence of the solution to the volume-preserving mean curvature flow for smooth initial data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(0)$$\end{document}A(0). They additionally proved that if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(0)$$\end{document}A(0) is sufficiently close to a sphere in a suitable sense, then there exists a global solution and it converges to some sphere as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}t→∞. Nevertheless, for more general smooth initial data singularities may also appear in finite time, even in the case of planar curves [41]. To be able to describe the evolution through these singularities, several authors have been interested in finding suitable notions of weak solutions. Mugnai, Seis and Spadaro [43] constructed solutions using an almost volume-preserving version of the scheme considered by Luckhaus and Sturzenhecker [39]. The main drawback is that this result is only conditional to the energy convergence assumption1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^T\mathcal {H}^{d-1}(\partial ^*\mathscr {A}^k(t))\,\text {d} t\rightarrow \int _0^T\mathcal {H}^{d-1}(\partial ^* \mathscr {A}(t)) \,\text {d} t \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0THd-1(∂∗Ak(t))dt→∫0THd-1(∂∗A(t))dtas \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}k→∞, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^*$$\end{document}∂∗ is the reduced boundary of the set, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{\mathscr {A}^k(t)\}_{t\in [0,T)}$$\end{document}{Ak(t)}t∈[0,T) is the time-discretized approximate solution to (1.1). This is a reasonable assumption ruling out the piling up of layers of surfaces, and thus it guarantees that the limit varifold preserves unit density. On the other hand, in general this property is hard to prove, since the framework of sets of finite perimeter intrinsically lacks compactness, and (1.3) can so far only be shown for some specific two-phase cases, as for instance when the scalar mean curvature H is nonnegative (see, e.g., [15]). Convergence assumptions as in (1.3) are quite common in the literature, and have been adopted, for instance, by Laux and Simon [37] to show the convergence of the nonlocal multi-phase Allen–Cahn equation to the volume-preserving multi-phase mean curvature flow. As a different notion of weak solution, although volume-preserving mean curvature flow does not satisfy a comparison principle, Kim and Kwon [33] proved the existence of a viscosity solution to (1.1), but only for the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(0)$$\end{document}A(0) satisfies a specific geometric condition called \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ-reflection. Nevertheless, this notion, albeit unique in the classical (nonpreserving) mean curvature flow [12, 19], for the case of volume-preserving mean curvature flow is still not known to be unique (see [33]).

Using yet another notion of weak solution, volume-preserving mean curvature flow can also be formulated for evolving varifolds as an extension of Brakke’s notion [11] of mean curvature flow to the volume-preserving case. This notion has the important advantage that it is not conditional to any energy convergence assumption. Takasao [46] first showed that solutions to a slightly modified version of the nonlocal Allen–Cahn equation due to Golovaty [28] converge to this varifold solution. More recently, the same author [47] refined his methods by relaxing the volume constraint in the approximation and only recovering the precise volume preservation in the sharp-interface limit. This allowed him to extend his result [46] to higher dimensions. Furthermore, in the recent paper [13] the authors extend the result in [47] by showing that such solution satisfies a suitable Brakke inequality in the case of periodic boundary conditions. Observe that Brakke flow is an appropriate weak solution since it is also well suited for analyzing the multi-phase mean curvature flow.

In this contribution we propose a new weak solution concept for volume-preserving mean curvature flow, which is the first to enjoy both unconditional existence and weak–strong uniqueness. This notion exploits the gradient-flow structure of the flow, mirroring the concepts known for general gradient flows as introduced by De Giorgi [6, 14], and Sandier and Serfaty [44] (see also [9]). Indeed, note that the volume-preserving mean curvature flow has a gradient-flow structure, as it is seen from the energy dissipation relation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\text {d} }{\text {d} t}\mathcal {H}^{d-1}(\partial \mathscr {A}(t))=\int _{\partial \mathscr {A}(t)}{\textbf {V}} \cdot {\textbf {H}} \text {d} \mathcal {H}^{d-1}=-\int _{\partial \mathscr {A}(t)}\left| {\textbf {V}} \right| ^2\text {d} \mathcal {H}^{d-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtHd-1(∂A(t))=∫∂A(t)V·HdHd-1=-∫∂A(t)V2dHd-1.This weak notion of solution has been first introduced in the pioneering [29] for the standard (nonpreserving) mean curvature flow, and our aim is here to show that this notion is flexible enough to incorporate the volume-preserving constraint.

As our first main result, in the present work we prove the existence of a global weak solution in this new sense, without any assumption on the energy convergence as (1.3), when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Ω=Td. Namely, we show that solutions to the same modified nonlocal Allen–Cahn equation used by Takasao [47], up to subsequences, converge to solutions of our new solution concept as the interface parameter vanishes. Our approach is based on [47], which is itself rooted in the fundamental work of Ilmanen [32]. Since the existence proof relies on the maximum principle, it is so far limited, as in [29], to the two-phase volume-preserving mean curvature flow. Nevertheless, the notion of De Giorgi-type varifold solution can be still extended to multi-phase flows (see [29]), even though so far the existence proof is not yet available.

As a final comment, we point out that varifold solution concepts are nowadays well established in the literature of geometric flows. For instance, Abels proved the existence of varifold solutions to the two-phase Navier–Stokes equation with surface tension [2]. More recently, a gradient flow approach à la De Giorgi, taking inspiration from [29], has been extended to treat varifold solutions to Mullins–Sekerka flow [30], as well as in the case of Navier–Stokes–Mullins–Sekerka equations [3].

In the second part of the paper, which deals either with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Ω=Rd or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Ω=Td, we show that the De Giorgi varifold solution we introduced does not allow for unphysical non-uniqueness before the appearance of singularities. In particular, we show that as long as a classical solution to volume-preserving mean curvature flow exists, any De Giorgi-type varifold solution in the sense of the present work coincides with this classical flow. The proof of this second result is based on a relative entropy approach.

First, we need to extend the notion of gradient-flow calibrations introduced in the recent work by Fischer, Hensel, Laux and Simon [22] to the volume-preserving case and show that any sufficiently regular classical solution is calibrated in this sense (see Theorem 3.11). The same task has already been performed in [34, 36] in different ways. The main technical issue is the extension B of the velocity field in the definition of gradient-flow calibrations. In place of the trivial ad hoc extension by nearest-point projection onto the classical solution, in [36] Laux solves a Neumann–Laplace equation to ensure that B also satisfies the incompressibility condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathop \text {div} B=0$$\end{document}divB=0, at most with a linear error moving away from the (smooth) interface. This is enough to show a weak–strong uniqueness principle in the class of distributional solutions to volume-preserving mean curvature flow [36]. On the other hand, in [34] the authors need to relax the divergence constraint of B, in order to additionally fix the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}\otimes {\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}$$\end{document}n∂A⊗n∂A component of the Jacobian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla B$$\end{document}∇B. To this aim they add a tangential part to the velocity field of the interface, so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=({\textbf {V}} \cdot {\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}){\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}+X$$\end{document}B=(V·n∂A)n∂A+X on the interface, where X is a nontrivial tangential vector field. This modification of the calibration allows the authors to prove a quantitative convergence result of the nonlocal Allen–Cahn equation, in the formulation of Golovaty [28], to the volume-preserving mean curvature flow.

Nevertheless, both the calibrations have the main drawback that the vector field B in general does not point in the normal direction on the interface. On the other hand this property is necessary in the proof of weak–strong uniqueness of our new De Giorgi-type varifold solution concept, since we want to consider a truly general (oriented) varifold in our weak solution notion, without any assumption on its integrality. Indeed, to find weak–strong uniqueness without asking B to be normal on the interface we would need to apply Brakke’s orthogonality theorem [11] to the weak solution, which is only possible for an integral varifold. Therefore, we propose a third gradient-flow calibration of the volume-preserving mean curvature flow (see Definition 3.10). More precisely, we construct B such that it solves an incompressible Stokes problem with nonhomogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions in the domain enclosed by the interface, so that it automatically satisfies the incompressibility constraint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathop \text {div} B=0$$\end{document}divB=0. Not only this, but the Dirichlet boundary conditions allow to directly impose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B=({\textbf {V}} \cdot {\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}){\textbf {n}} _{\partial \mathscr {A}}$$\end{document}B=(V·n∂A)n∂A on the interface, giving the desired direction of B pointing in the normal direction on the interface. Surprisingly enough, in the context of gradient-flow calibrations, this is the first time that the vector field B is assumed to directly solve a vectorial elliptic problem rather than to be a (differential) transformation of a solution to a scalar elliptic one (like, e.g., the gradient of the solution to a scalar Neumann–Laplace equation). Our second main result of the present contribution is thus the proof that any sufficiently smooth classical solution is calibrated in this new sense.

Making use of this new gradient-flow calibration, we can now pass to our third result, i.e., the weak–strong uniqueness of our new De Giorgi varifold solution concept, which is proven through a relative entropy technique. The basic idea of this approach is to construct a quantity which on one side captures the distance between a weak and a classical solution in a sufficiently strong sense, and on the other side allows for an estimate on its time evolution. This is the relative entropy (see the definition in (3.22)). This approach was recently widely adopted to study geometric evolution equations. For example, Fischer and Hensel showed in [20] a weak–strong uniqueness principle for Navier–Stokes two-phase flow with surface tension based on a suitable relative entropy functional. Other examples are the above mentioned proof of weak–strong uniqueness for distributional solutions to volume-preserving mean curvature flow by Laux [36], as well as the recent result on the weak–strong uniqueness of two dimensional Mullins–Sekerka flow [21] in the case of the De Giorgi varifold solutions introduced in [30].

The method can also be used to prove quantitative convergence of the Allen–Cahn equation to mean curvature flow as shown by Fischer, Laux and Simon [23], see also [24]. As already discussed, this method has also been extended in [34] to the convergence of a suitable nonlocal Allen–Cahn equation to a volume-preserving mean curvature flow. Also, the extension of the relative entropy technique to sufficiently regular, strongly convex anisotropies in both surface tension and mobility function is possible, and we refer, for instance, to [17, 35, 38]. We also refer to [1] for the extension of the arguments to some non-isothermal models.

The strength of this method is that one can also lower the assumptions on the weak solution, say as long as it satisfies a suitable optimal energy-dissipation relation. This is the case of the weak–strong uniqueness principle for De Giorgi varifold solutions to both 2D Mullins–Sekerka flow [30] and mean curvature flow [29], and it highlights the importance of a gradient flow structure. In our third main result we thus follow the relative entropy approach to obtain a weak–strong uniqueness principle for our De Giorgi-type varifold solution to volume-preserving mean curvature flow, extending on one side the result of [29] to this more involved case, and on the other side lifting the weak–strong uniqueness result of [36] to the varifold setting. We point out that this is the first (weak–strong) uniqueness principle for volume-preserving mean curvature flow which relies on both a weak and strong solution whose existence is unconditionally proven. In the case of the standard (nonpreserving) mean curvature flow Stuvard and Tonegawa [45] have shown the existence of a weak solution, called the canonical Brakke flow, satisfying a transport equation in addition to Brakke’s family of inequalities, and thus enjoying further regularity properties, including weak–strong uniqueness. Nonetheless this is not the case for the volume-preserving mean curvature flow, for which to the best of our knowledge the notion of solution which is closer to a canonical Brakke flow is the one proposed in [13], which so far is not enough to entail any weak–strong uniqueness principle. Not only this, but our result is in the direction of finding a minimal solution concept that brings sufficient information to uniquely describe volume-preserving mean curvature flow, whereas the notion of solution proposed in [45], even if fully extended to the volume-preserving case, would be more regular than our new notion of solution à la De Giorgi.

Structure of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2 we give some preliminary notation which will be used in the sequel. Then Sect. 3 is devoted to the presentation and discussion of the three main results. Starting with the sections dedicated to the proofs, in Sect. 4 we prove Theorem 3.5, whereas in Sect. 5 we show the proof of Lemma 3.9. In Sect. 6 we prove the existence of the novel gradient-flow calibration for sufficiently smooth volume-preserving mean curvature flow, which leads to Sect. 7, dedicated to the proof of Theorem 3.12. In conclusion, in Appendix A we give an alternative simpler proof of Theorem 3.12 under an additional assumption of integrality of the weak solution varifold.

For a locally compact and separable metric space X, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {M}(X)$$\end{document}M(X) the space of (finite) Radon measures on X. Lebesgue and Hausdorff measures. Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2,3$$\end{document}d=2,3 or the d-dimensional flat torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Td. Given a Borel set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R, we also set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^1(I)$$\end{document}L1(I) to be the corresponding one-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

Lebesgue and Hausdorff measures. Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω to be \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=2,3$$\end{document}d=2,3 or the d-dimensional flat torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Td. Given a Borel set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\subset \Omega $$\end{document}A⊂Ω, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^d(A)$$\end{document}Ld(A) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {H}^{d-1}(A)$$\end{document}Hd-1(A) the d-dimensional Lebesgue measure of A and its \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(d-1)$$\end{document}(d-1)-dimensional Hausdorff measure, respectively. Given a Borel set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I\subset \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}I⊂R, we also set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}^1(I)$$\end{document}L1(I) to be the corresponding one-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

Notation for general Banach spaces. For any normed space X of scalar-valued functions, we denote its norm by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _X$$\end{document}‖·‖X and its dual space by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X'$$\end{document}X′. Given any vector space X, corresponding spaces of vector-valued or matrix-valued functions with each component belonging to X are denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {X}} $$\end{document}X.

Notation for general Banach spaces. For any normed space X of scalar-valued functions, we denote its norm by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \cdot \Vert _X$$\end{document}‖·‖X and its dual space by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X'$$\end{document}X′. Recalling (see, e.g., [8]) that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BV(\Omega )$$\end{document}BV(Ω) is the dual of a separable Banach space X, we obtain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty _{w*}(0,T;BV(\Omega ))=(L^1(0,T;X))'$$\end{document}Lw∗∞(0,T;BV(Ω))=(L1(0,T;X))′ and that uniformly bounded sets in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^\infty _{w*}(0,T;BV(\Omega ))$$\end{document}Lw∗∞(0,T;BV(Ω)) are weakly* compact.

Spaces of continuous functions. For Banach spaces X and Y, C(X; Y) is the space of continuous functions mapping from X to Y. Then, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_c^k(X;Y)$$\end{document}Cck(X;Y) the spaces of k-times continuously differentiable functions with compact support with values in Y. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y=\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}Y=R we omit its dependence.

Spaces of continuous functions. For Banach spaces X and Y, C(X; Y) is the space of continuous functions mapping from X to Y. Then, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_c^k(X;Y)$$\end{document}Cck(X;Y) the spaces of k-times continuously differentiable functions with compact support with values in Y. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y=\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}Y=R we omit its dependence.

Main results on De Giorgi-type varifold solutions for volume-preserving mean curvature flow

Definition, existence results, and consistency

We first extend the novel weak solution concept for two-phase mean curvature flow introduced in [29] to the case of volume-preserving mean curvature flow. Formally, given a smoothly evolving open set A(t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} Notice that the scalar normal velocity V and the mean curvature vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {H}} $$\end{document}H, as well as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ, can have very low regularity and they are suitable to be defined in a weak setting. Since the evolution of A(t) is assumed to be smooth, the volume rate of change satisfies3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt} \mathcal {L}^d(A(t)) \underbrace{=}_{(1)} -\int _{\partial A(t)} V \text {d} \mathcal {H}^{d-1}\underbrace{=}_{(2)}0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtLd(A(t))=⏟(1)-∫∂A(t)VdHd-1=⏟(2)0,where here and in the sequel we use the sign convention \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V>0$$\end{document}V>0 for a shrinking set A(t), say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {V}} =V{\textbf {n}} $$\end{document}V=Vn. First, from identity (1) in (3.2), the resulting localized PDE formulated using the characteristic function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (x,t)=\chi _{A(t)}(x)$$\end{document}χ(x,t)=χA(t)(x) then reads\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t \chi + V |\nabla \chi | =0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tχ+V|∇χ|=0,so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi $$\end{document}χ is transported by the vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {V}} =V {\textbf {n}} $$\end{document}V=Vn, recalling that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {n}} =\frac{\nabla \chi }{|\nabla \chi |}$$\end{document}n=∇χ|∇χ|. By substituting the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\nabla \chi |$$\end{document}|∇χ|, which would be lost in the limit in the varifold sense, the authors of [29] define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t \chi + V \omega =0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tχ+Vω=0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω is the mass measure of the limit varifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ, and V is measurable with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω, and not necessarily \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\nabla \chi |$$\end{document}|∇χ|. This gives rise to a new notion of normal velocity, which will be exploited also in the present contribution.

We come now to identity (2) in (3.2). This has already been observed in (1.2), and it is the main property of the volume-preserving mean curvature flow, since it entails volume conservation in the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathcal {L}^d({A(t)})=\mathcal {L}^d(A(0)), $$\end{document}Ld(A(t))=Ld(A(0)),for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0. Clearly, to give a meaning to our new notion of solution, this property has to be satisfied also by the weak solution we aim at defining.

We give now our new definition of weak solution for (two-phase) volume-preserving mean curvature flow.

(Volume conservation) The total volume is preserved, in the sense that 3.3e\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\Omega } \chi (x,t)\,\text {d} x=\int _{\Omega }\chi _0(x)\,\text {d} x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ωχ(x,t)dx=∫Ωχ0(x)dx, for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0.

(Volume conservation) The total volume is preserved, in the sense that 3.3e\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\Omega } \chi (x,t)\,\text {d} x=\int _{\Omega }\chi _0(x)\,\text {d} x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ωχ(x,t)dx=∫Ωχ0(x)dx, for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0.

Remark 3.2

(Rectifiability of the varifold) Since, by the definition of varifold solution given above, the mean curvature vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {H}} \in L^2_{loc}((0,\infty );L^2(\Omega ,d\omega _t;\mathbb {R}^d))$$\end{document}H∈Lloc2((0,∞);L2(Ω,dωt;Rd)), it also holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _\Omega \left| {\textbf {H}} \right| ^2\text {d} \omega _t<+\infty $$\end{document}∫ΩH2dωt<+∞ for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0. Then, Allard’s rectifiability criterion [5] allows to conclude that the unoriented varifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{\mu }}_t$$\end{document}μ^t, corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _t$$\end{document}μt (identifying antipodal points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm p$$\end{document}±p on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Sd-1), is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(d-1)$$\end{document}(d-1)-rectifiable for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}t∈(0,∞). Notice that in general we do not know anything about the integer rectifiability of the varifold. Nevertheless, we will see that Definition 3.1 is enough to guarantee the existence of a solution and a weak–strong uniqueness principle without resorting to the integrality of the varifold (to apply Brakke’s orthogonality theorem [11]), in perfect analogy with the standard (nonpreserving) mean curvature flow studied in [29].

Remark 3.3

(Local time integrability of the mean curvature vector. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {H}} $$\end{document}H) Observe that, differently from the weak solution notion in [29], here we can only require the generalized mean curvature to be locally integrable in time, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {H}} \in L^2_{loc}((0,\infty );L^2(\Omega ,d\omega _t;\mathbb {R}^d))$$\end{document}H∈Lloc2((0,∞);L2(Ω,dωt;Rd)), due to the presence of the locally integrable Lagarange multiplier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \in L^2_{loc}(0,\infty )$$\end{document}λ∈Lloc2(0,∞) in the De Giorgi inequality (3.3d).

We now show that any limit as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} The standard Allen–Cahn equation is the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-gradient flow of the Modica–Mortola energy3.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_\varepsilon ^S(u) := \int _{\Omega } \left( \frac{\varepsilon }{2}|\nabla u|^2 + \frac{1}{\varepsilon }W(u)\right) dx. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EεS(u):=∫Ωε2|∇u|2+1εW(u)dx.On the other hand, in this modified Allen–Cahn equation, proposed in [28], the total energy is a modified version of the one above, taking into account the relaxed volume constraint. Namely, we need to define the additional energy3.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E^P_\varepsilon (u):=\frac{1}{2\varepsilon ^\alpha }\left( \int _\Omega (\phi (u(x,0))-\phi (u(x,t)))\,\text {d} x\right) ^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EεP(u):=12εα∫Ω(ϕ(u(x,0))-ϕ(u(x,t)))dx2,and, setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\varepsilon :=E_\varepsilon ^S+E_\varepsilon ^P$$\end{document}Eε:=EεS+EεP, observe that (3.8) is indeed an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 gradient flow, thanks to the identity\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt} E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon (\,\cdot \,,t)) = - According to the approach looking for an energy dissipation inequality à la De Giorgi (see (3.1)), here we need to measure the energy dissipation in the balanced way3.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{d}{dt} E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon (\,\cdot \,,t))&= -\frac{1}{2} \int _{\Omega } \varepsilon (\partial _tu_\varepsilon (\,\cdot \,,t))^2 \,\text {d} x\nonumber \\&\quad - \frac{1}{2}\int _{\Omega } \frac{1}{\varepsilon }\left( \varepsilon \Delta u_\varepsilon (\,\cdot \,,t) - \frac{1}{\varepsilon }W'(u_\varepsilon (\,\cdot \,,t)+\lambda _\varepsilon \sqrt{2W(u_\varepsilon (\cdot ,t))}) \right) ^2 \,\text {d} x, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtEε(uε(·,t))=-12∫Ωε(∂tuε(·,t))2dx-12∫Ω1εεΔuε(·,t)-1εW′(uε(·,t)+λε2W(uε(·,t)))2dx,which comes from (3.8) by straightforward computations.

Our first main result is the following, where we need to restrict ourselves to the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Ω=Td, since the proof is strongly based on the seminal work of [47], which is developed only for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d-$$\end{document}d-dimensional flat torus with periodic boundary conditions to (3.8), although we expect a similar proof to hold for the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Ω=Rd, as it is for the standard Allen–Cahn equation in Ilmanen’s work [32]. To be precise, the results in [47] have been obtained for a double well potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W(u)=\frac{1}{2}(1-u^2)$$\end{document}W(u)=12(1-u2), with wells in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm 1$$\end{document}±1, but it is immediate to see that the same results hold for the choice of the same (up to a rescaling) potential with the two wells shifted in 0, 1. We prefer to consider the wells in 0, 1 to be coherent with the notation in [29], which we extend to the volume-preserving case. In the following theorem we consider a specific (rather technical) class of well-prepared initial data as in [47], whose precise introduction will be in Definition 4.3 in Sect. 4, for the sake of readability.

Remark 3.6

The validity of the De Giorgi-type inequality on (s, t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}t∈(0,∞), is here not conditional to some additional assumption on the second moments of the initial datum, as in [29, Theorem 1], since in this case we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Ω=Td and not \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Rd.

Remark 3.7

(Brakke’s orthogonality theorem) Notice that, since the varifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _t$$\end{document}μt constructed in Theorem 3.5 additionally enjoys integrality, we can apply the well-known Brakke’s orthogonality of the mean curvature vector (see [11, Chapter 5]) and deduce, as a straightforward consequence, that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {H}} $$\end{document}H points in the same direction of the expected value \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\langle \pi _{x,t}\rangle $$\end{document}⟨πx,t⟩ (if this does not vanish), namely3.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vert \langle \pi _{x,t}\rangle \vert ^2{\textbf {H}} (x,t)=(\langle \pi _{x,t}\rangle \cdot {\textbf {H}} (x,t))\langle \pi _{x,t}\rangle , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|⟨πx,t⟩|2H(x,t)=(⟨πx,t⟩·H(x,t))⟨πx,t⟩,for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _t$$\end{document}ωt-almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \Omega $$\end{document}x∈Ω and almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0. As already pointed out in Remark 3.2, rectifiability of the varifold cannot in general be deduced for De Giorgi-type varifold solutions according to Definition 3.1.

In addition to Theorem 3.5, as in [29] we can also show the consistency of the varifold solution given in Definition 3.1 with respect to the classical solution to the volume-preserving mean curvature flow, which we now define. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\text {strong} }\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}Tstrong∈(0,∞) be a finite time horizon, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}=(\mathscr {A}(t))_{t \in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]}$$\end{document}A=(A(t))t∈[0,Tstrong] be a family of bounded domains in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω, smoothly evolving, where the associated family of interfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}=(\mathcal {I}(t))_{t \in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]}$$\end{document}I=(I(t))t∈[0,Tstrong] is assumed to evolve smoothly following a volume-preserving mean curvature flow, in the sense of the following

Gradient-flow calibration and weak–strong uniqueness

We now address the weak–strong uniqueness of De Giorgi-type varifold solutions for (two-phase) volume-preserving mean curvature flow. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu ,\chi )$$\end{document}(μ,χ) be a De Giorgi-type varifold solution according to Definition 3.1.

To measure the distance between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}$$\end{document}A and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu ,\chi )$$\end{document}(μ,χ) we exploit the relative entropy3.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E[\mu ,\chi |\mathscr {A}](t):= \int _{\Omega {\times } \mathbb {S}^{d-1}} 1 - p \cdot \xi (x,t) \text {d} \mu _t(x,p) \ge 0, \quad t \in [0,T_{\text {strong} }], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[μ,χ|A](t):=∫Ω×Sd-11-p·ξ(x,t)dμt(x,p)≥0,t∈[0,Tstrong],together with the bulk error3.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_{\text {bulk} }[\chi |\mathscr {A}](t):= \int _{\Omega } |\chi _{A(t)}(x) {-} \chi _{\mathscr {A}(t)}(x)| |\vartheta (x,t)| \,\text {d} x\ge 0, \quad t \in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ebulk[χ|A](t):=∫Ω|χA(t)(x)-χA(t)(x)||ϑ(x,t)|dx≥0,t∈[0,Tstrong].We point out that in the last definition there is a slight inconsistency in the notation, since to be precise one should write the left-hand side as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {bulk} }[\chi _A|\chi _{\mathscr {A}}](t)$$\end{document}Ebulk[χA|χA](t). This is done for consistency with the relative entropy functional used in (3.22). The vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ appearing in the definition (3.22) is a suitable extension of the normal vector field associated with the classical solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}$$\end{document}A (pointing inward), whereas the weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\vartheta |$$\end{document}|ϑ| is a smooth version of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t) \mapsto \min \{1,\text {dist} (x,\mathcal {I}(t))\}$$\end{document}(x,t)↦min{1,dist(x,I(t))}, which disappears on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)$$\end{document}I(t). For a rigorous construction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi ,\vartheta )$$\end{document}(ξ,ϑ) here we introduce a new notion of gradient-flow calibrations in the context of volume-preserving mean curvature flow, which differs from the one first introduced in [36]. Indeed, to complete the weak–strong uniqueness argument we further need that the vector field B, approximating the velocity, is normal to the interface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}I. This is a completely nontrivial property which is new of this contribution. In particular, we will show that the flow admits a gradient-flow calibration \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi ,B,\vartheta , \lambda ^*)$$\end{document}(ξ,B,ϑ,λ∗) in the following sense:

Definition 3.10

(Novel gradient-flow calibration for volume-preserving mean curvature flow) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{strong}>0$$\end{document}Tstrong>0 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}=(\mathscr {A}(t))_{t \in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]}$$\end{document}A=(A(t))t∈[0,Tstrong] be a family of bounded domains in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω, say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(t)\subset B_{R_*}(0)$$\end{document}A(t)⊂BR∗(0) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T_{strong}]$$\end{document}t∈[0,Tstrong], for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_*>0$$\end{document}R∗>0. Let also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)=\partial \mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}I(t)=∂A(t). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi ,B:\Omega \times [0,T_{\text {strong} }]$$\end{document}ξ,B:Ω×[0,Tstrong], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta :\Omega \times [0,T_{\text {strong} }]\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ϑ:Ω×[0,Tstrong]→R, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^*:[0,T_{\text {strong} }]\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}λ∗:[0,Tstrong]→R. (i)The vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ, the scalar function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ, and the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^*$$\end{document}λ∗ are such that 3.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\xi \in C_c^{0,1}(\Omega \times [0,T_{\text {strong} }];\mathbb {R}^d),\nonumber \\&\vartheta \in C^{0,1}(\Omega \times [0,T_{\text {strong} }])\cap L^\infty (\Omega \times [0,T_{\text {strong} }]),\quad \lambda ^*\in L^2(0,T_{\text {strong} }). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ξ∈Cc0,1(Ω×[0,Tstrong];Rd),ϑ∈C0,1(Ω×[0,Tstrong])∩L∞(Ω×[0,Tstrong]),λ∗∈L2(0,Tstrong). (Sign and coercivity of the transported weight).
