All the quantities \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi ,B,\vartheta ,\lambda ^*)$$\end{document}(ξ,B,ϑ,λ∗) in the definition have immediate interpretation. Indeed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ is an extension of the normal vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {n}} _\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}nI, whereas, B is an extension of the velocity vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_\mathcal {I}{\textbf {n}} _\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}VInI, with the fundamental additional properties that it is solenoidal (which is compatible with the volume-preservation of the corresponding PDE in Definition 3.8). As already observed, the novelty in this gradient-flow calibration with respect to the one in [36] lies in the fact that here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B(\cdot ,t)$$\end{document}B(·,t) is also normal to the interface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)$$\end{document}I(t), i.e., it coincides with the velocity vector field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_\mathcal {I}{\textbf {n}} _\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}VInI on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)$$\end{document}I(t). Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \vartheta \right| $$\end{document}ϑ is simply a smooth version of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,t)\mapsto \min \{1,{\frac{1}{c}}\ \text {dist} (x,\mathcal {I}(t))\}$$\end{document}(x,t)↦min{1,1cdist(x,I(t))}, whereas \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^*$$\end{document}λ∗ is the analog of the Lagrange multiplier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda _\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}λI. Note that this calibration has the peculiar property that we do not need any time regularity of the flow B, although, as it is somehow hidden in the proofs in [34, 36], one needs that some norms of B are uniformly bounded in time.

Our second main result is that such a gradient-flow calibration of Definition 3.10 exists for any smooth volume-preserving mean curvature flow.

Theorem 3.11

(Existence of a gradient-flow calibration to volume-preserving mean curvature flow) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\text {strong} }\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}Tstrong∈(0,∞), and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {A}(t))_{t\in [0,T_{strong}]}$$\end{document}(A(t))t∈[0,Tstrong] be a smoothly evolving family of bounded domains (open and connected) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω whose associated family of interfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {I}(t))_{t\in [0,T_{strong}]}$$\end{document}(I(t))t∈[0,Tstrong], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)=\partial \mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}I(t)=∂A(t) evolves smoothly by a volume-preserving mean curvature flow as in Definition 3.8. Then there exists a tuple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi ,B,\vartheta ,\lambda ^*)$$\end{document}(ξ,B,ϑ,λ∗) which is a gradient-flow calibration for volume-preserving mean curvature flow as in Definition 3.10.

Surprisingly enough, as in [34, 36], also with this new calibration we do not need any estimate on the closeness of the respective Lagrange multipliers to deduce the weak–strong uniqueness result. As a byproduct this also gives a quantitative stability for De Giorgi-type varifold solutions as in Definition 3.1. We thus have our third main result as follows

Theorem 3.12

(Weak–strong uniqueness for De Giorgi-type varifold solutions) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\text {strong} }\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}Tstrong∈(0,∞) be a finite time horizon, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}=(\mathscr {A}_t)_{t\in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]}$$\end{document}A=(At)t∈[0,Tstrong] be a smoothly evolving family of bounded domains in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}Ω=Rd or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega =\mathbb {T}^d$$\end{document}Ω=Td, whose associated family of interfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {I}(t))_{t \in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]}$$\end{document}(I(t))t∈[0,Tstrong] evolves smoothly following a volume-preserving mean curvature flow as in Definition 3.8.

Let also \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu ,\chi )$$\end{document}(μ,χ) be a De Giorgi-type varifold solution for volume-preserving mean curvature flow with initial data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu _0,\chi _0)$$\end{document}(μ0,χ0) according to Definition 3.1. The following stability estimate holds3.40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&E[\mu ,\chi |\mathscr {A}](T)+E_{\text {bulk} }[\chi |\mathscr {A}](T) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[μ,χ|A](T)+Ebulk[χ|A](T)3.41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\quad \le C(T) (E[\mu _0,\chi _0|\mathscr {A}(0)]+E_{\text {bulk} }[\chi _0|\mathscr {A}(0)] ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}≤C(T)(E[μ0,χ0|A(0)]+Ebulk[χ0|A(0)])for some constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C=C(T)>0$$\end{document}C=C(T)>0 and for almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]$$\end{document}T∈[0,Tstrong].

More precisely, if it holds that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi _0=\chi _{\mathscr {A}(0)}$$\end{document}χ0=χA(0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _0 = |\nabla \chi _0|\otimes {\delta _{\frac{\nabla \chi _0}{|\nabla \chi _0|}}}$$\end{document}μ0=|∇χ0|⊗δ∇χ0|∇χ0|, then for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]$$\end{document}t∈[0,Tstrong] we have3.42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \chi (\cdot ,t)&= \chi _{\mathscr {A}(t)} \text { almost everywhere in } \Omega , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}χ(·,t)=χA(t)almost everywhere inΩ,3.43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _t&= |\nabla \chi (\cdot ,t)|\otimes (\delta _{\frac{\nabla \chi (\cdot ,t)}{|\nabla \chi (\cdot ,t)|}(x)})_{x\in \Omega }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μt=|∇χ(·,t)|⊗(δ∇χ(·,t)|∇χ(·,t)|(x))x∈Ω.

Remark 3.13

We point out that, due to the presence of the Lagrange multiplier \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda $$\end{document}λ, one cannot retrieve a stability estimate for the relative entropy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E[\mu ,\chi |\mathscr {A}]$$\end{document}E[μ,χ|A] decoupled from the stability estimate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\text {bulk} }[\chi |\mathscr {A}]$$\end{document}Ebulk[χ|A], differently from what happens with the standard mean curvature flow (see [29, Theorem 2]).

We will prove this Theorem in Sect. 7 by making use of the gradient-flow calibration given in Theorem 3.11. On the other hand, there are cases in which the essential property (3.30) of the extended velocity vector field B to be normal to the interface cannot be satisfied, as for instance in the gradient-flow calibration proposed in [34]. Also, in general we cannot expect a similar property for the gradient-flow calibration of a multi-phase mean curvature flow (due to the presence of moving triple junctions, see, e.g. [22, 29]). To deal with these cases, the price to pay for two-phase flows is simply a further assumption in the definition of the weak (varifold) solution, namely that the unoriented varifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{\mu }}_t$$\end{document}μ^t, corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _t$$\end{document}μt (identifying antipodal points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm p$$\end{document}±p on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Sd-1), has integer multiplicity for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}t∈(0,∞). This is not a limiting assumption, in the sense that the varifold solution constructed as a sharp interface limit in Theorem 3.5 is even integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(d-1)$$\end{document}(d-1)-rectifiable. In Appendix A we will thus prove the weak–strong uniqueness principle under this extra assumption on the integrality of the varifold in the definition of weak solution, and without making use of property (3.30) of the gradient-flow calibration.

The following compactness statement can be proved similarly to [29, Proposition 1], recalling [47, Lemma 1].

Remark 4.2

Takasao in [47, Proposition 9] actually proves more. Indeed, if we define4.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\widetilde{\omega }}_\varepsilon := \left( \left( \frac{\varepsilon }{2}\left| \nabla u_\varepsilon \right| ^2+\frac{1}{\varepsilon }W(u_\varepsilon )\right) \mathcal {L}^d\llcorner \Omega \right) \otimes \left( \mathcal {L}^1\llcorner (0,\infty )\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ω~ε:=ε2∇uε2+1εW(uε)Ld⌞Ω⊗L1⌞(0,∞),it holds4.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ({\widetilde{\omega }}_{\varepsilon })_t{\mathop {\rightharpoonup }\limits ^{*}}\widetilde{\omega }_t\quad \text {weakly* as Radon measures in } \Omega , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(ω~ε)t⇀∗ω~tweakly* as Radon measures inΩ,as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0, for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}t≥0, and for some Radon measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widetilde{\omega }}_t$$\end{document}ω~t. This convergence is not strictly necessary for the proof of Proposition 4.1, but it is fundamental to prove the last part of Theorem 3.5. Indeed, as already observed in Remark 3.6, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω is the d-dimensional flat torus, it immediately holds4.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ({\widetilde{\omega }}_{\varepsilon })_t(\Omega )\rightarrow {\widetilde{\omega }}_t(\Omega ), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(ω~ε)t(Ω)→ω~t(Ω),as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0, for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}t≥0, and this is necessary to pass to the limit in the energy inequality for generic time instants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le s\le t$$\end{document}0≤s≤t. We refer to Lemma 4.5 for more details about this convergence.

Proof

We only give a sketch of the immediate results, since the only nontrivial one, i.e., (4.13), can be found in [47, Proposition 1], to which we refer the interested reader. Now, (4.11) directly comes from (4.5), and, by the usual Modica–Mortola/Bogomol’nyi trick, this time applied to the time derivative of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _\varepsilon $$\end{document}ψε, we also have, exploiting (4.3),4.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^T\int _\Omega \vert \partial _t\psi _\varepsilon \vert \,\text {d} x\,\text {d} t&\le \sqrt{2}\left( \int _0^T\int _\Omega \frac{1}{\varepsilon }W(u_\varepsilon )\,\text {d} x\,\text {d} t\right) ^\frac{1}{2}\left( \int _0^T\int _\Omega \varepsilon \vert \partial _t u_\varepsilon \vert ^2\,\text {d} x\,\text {d} t\right) ^{\frac{1}{2}} \nonumber \\ &\le \int _0^T\int _\Omega \frac{2}{\varepsilon }W(u_\varepsilon )\,\text {d} x\,\text {d} t+\int _0^T\int _\Omega \varepsilon \vert \partial _t u_\varepsilon \vert ^2\,\text {d} x\,\text {d} t\nonumber \\&\le \int _0^T2E^S_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon )+\int _0^T\int _\Omega \varepsilon \vert \partial _t u_\varepsilon \vert ^2\,\text {d} x\,\text {d} t\nonumber \\ &\le (2T+1)E_\varepsilon (u_{\varepsilon ,0})\le (2T+1)E_0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0T∫Ω|∂tψε|dxdt≤2∫0T∫Ω1εW(uε)dxdt12∫0T∫Ωε|∂tuε|2dxdt12≤∫0T∫Ω2εW(uε)dxdt+∫0T∫Ωε|∂tuε|2dxdt≤∫0T2EεS(uε)+∫0T∫Ωε|∂tuε|2dxdt≤(2T+1)Eε(uε,0)≤(2T+1)E0,for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>0$$\end{document}T>0, immediately giving (4.12). As (4.13) is already obtained in [47, Proposition 1], we pass to show the convergences. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _\varepsilon $$\end{document}ψε is uniformly bounded in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$BV(\Omega \times (0,T))$$\end{document}BV(Ω×(0,T)) for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>0$$\end{document}T>0, by compactness we infer (4.14). \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0, converges to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {L}^d(A(t))-\mathcal {L}^d(A_0))$$\end{document}(Ld(A(t))-Ld(A0)) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0, we infer (4.17). In conclusion, convergences (4.18)–(4.17) are immediate from the definitions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _\varepsilon $$\end{document}ωε and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _\varepsilon $$\end{document}με, by weak* compactness recalling (4.11). This concludes the proof. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The following proposition can be found in [47], and is rooted in the result of Ilmanen [32] based on the maximum principle. Namely, if one chooses in a suitable way the initial data, then the discrepancy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{\varepsilon }{2}|\nabla u_\varepsilon (x,t)|^2 - \frac{1}{\varepsilon }W(u_\varepsilon (x,t))$$\end{document}ε2|∇uε(x,t)|2-1εW(uε(x,t)) between the two terms of the energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^S_\varepsilon $$\end{document}EεS will remain nonpositive for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}t≥0. This is well known from [32] in the standard Allen–Cahn equation, but it is not in general so trivial for other problems, as in our modified nonlocal Allen–Cahn equation. Additionally, the discrepancy vanishes in the sense of measures:4.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \frac{\varepsilon }{2}|\nabla u_\varepsilon |^2 - \frac{1}{\varepsilon }W(u_\varepsilon )\right) \mathcal {L}^d\otimes \mathcal {L}^1 {\mathop {\rightharpoonup }\limits ^{*}} 0 \quad \text {weakly* as Radon measures}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ε2|∇uε|2-1εW(uε)Ld⊗L1⇀∗0weakly* as Radon measures,meaning that there is equipartition of energy as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0. Proof

First, notice that, from (4.27) and since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W(u)=18 u^2(u-1)^2\ge \frac{72}{5}u^4$$\end{document}W(u)=18u2(u-1)2≥725u4, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u>M$$\end{document}u>M, M sufficiently large, it holds4.37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\int _\Omega u_{\varepsilon ,0}^4\,\text {d} x\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ωuε,04dx4.38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\quad \le \int _{\left| u_{\varepsilon ,0} \right| \le M}M^3u_{\varepsilon ,0}\,\text {d} x+\int _{\left| u_{\varepsilon ,0} \right|> M} \frac{5}{72}W(u_{\varepsilon ,0})\,\text {d} x\le C,\quad \forall \varepsilon >0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}≤∫uε,0≤MM3uε,0dx+∫uε,0>M572W(uε,0)dx≤C,∀ε>0,i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_{\varepsilon ,0}$$\end{document}uε,0 is uniformly bounded in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4(\Omega )$$\end{document}L4(Ω). Also, from (4.29) we deduce that, up to a nonrelabeled subsequence,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u_{\varepsilon ,0}\rightarrow \chi _{U_0}, \quad \text {almost everywhere in }\Omega , $$\end{document}uε,0→χU0,almost everywhere inΩ,as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0, and, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi :s\mapsto \int _0^s\sqrt{2W(\tau )}\text {d} \tau $$\end{document}ϕ:s↦∫0s2W(τ)dτ is (Lipschitz) continuous, we also have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _\varepsilon (\cdot ,0)=\phi (u_{\varepsilon ,0}(\cdot ))\rightarrow \chi _{U_0}=\psi (x,0)$$\end{document}ψε(·,0)=ϕ(uε,0(·))→χU0=ψ(x,0) almost everyhere in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω. Then we have, for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M>0$$\end{document}M>0 sufficiently large, recalling (4.38),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _\Omega \left| \psi _\varepsilon (x,0) \right| \,\text {d} x\le \,&\int _{\{\left| u_{\varepsilon ,0}>M \right| \}}C\left| u_{\varepsilon ,0}(x) \right| ^3\,\text {d} x\\&+Lip({\phi _{|[0,M]}})\int _\Omega \left| u_{\varepsilon ,0}(x) \right| \,\text {d} x\le C<+\infty , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ωψε(x,0)dx≤∫{uε,0>M}Cuε,0(x)3dx+Lip(ϕ|[0,M])∫Ωuε,0(x)dx≤C<+∞,so that, also applying the Modica–Mortola/Bogomol’nyi trick by means of (4.27), we infer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _\varepsilon (\cdot ,0)\in W^{1,1}(\Omega )\hookrightarrow L^{\frac{d}{d-1}}(\Omega )$$\end{document}ψε(·,0)∈W1,1(Ω)↪Ldd-1(Ω) uniformly in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{d}{d-1}>1$$\end{document}dd-1>1, this allows to apply Vitali’s Convergence Theorem and deduce that, up to a subsequence, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _\varepsilon (\cdot ,0)\rightarrow \psi (x,0)$$\end{document}ψε(·,0)→ψ(x,0) strongly in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^1(\Omega )$$\end{document}L1(Ω) as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0. Notice that, by uniqueness of the limit, this also holds for the entire sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε, not only up to subsequences, entailing (4.31).

Proceeding in the proof, convergence (4.26) can be easily deduced from property (4.28) of the initial data, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^S_\varepsilon (u_{\varepsilon ,0})={\widetilde{\omega }}_{\varepsilon ,0}(\Omega )$$\end{document}EεS(uε,0)=ω~ε,0(Ω). Then property (4.33) is shown in [47, Proposition 6] based on maximum principle, whereas inequality (4.34) can be immediately inferred from the energy inequality (4.3). In conclusion, (4.35) corresponds to the vanishing discrepancy measure (4.25), whose proof can be found in [47, Theorem 13], and it is crucially based on the fundamental result in [32]. Then (4.36) can easily be obtained exactly as in [29, Proof of Proposition 2], by slightly manipulating (4.35). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We now aim at showing that the time slices of the measure \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω defined in Proposition 4.1 are (sharply) bounded above by the total energy. This is analogous to [29, Lemma 2]. In this case it is slight more delicate, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\varepsilon ^S(u_\varepsilon (\cdot ,t))$$\end{document}EεS(uε(·,t)) is not monotone nonincreasing in time, since this property is enjoyed by the total energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon )$$\end{document}Eε(uε). This is actually not an issue since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^P_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon )\ge 0$$\end{document}EεP(uε)≥0. One could wonder if the inequality is actually sharp, due to the presence of the energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\varepsilon ^P$$\end{document}EεP, and the answer is indeed affirmative, since the inequality is even an equality. Additionally, in this Lemma we also prove the compatibility condition (3.3f). In particular, we have

Proof

The proof follows the lines of [29, Lemma 2] (see also [47, Proof of Proposition 10] for a similar argument). First, notice that, due to the energy inequality (4.4), we infer that, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon >0$$\end{document}ε>0, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon (\cdot ,t))$$\end{document}t↦Eε(uε(·,t)) is monotonically nonincreasing, and also, recalling (4.34), uniformly bounded for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\ge 0$$\end{document}t≥0. As a consequence, by Helly’s Selection Theorem there exists a subsequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0 and a nonincreasing function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto E(t)$$\end{document}t↦E(t) such that4.42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon (\cdot ,t))\rightarrow E(t),\quad \forall t\ge 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Eε(uε(·,t))→E(t),∀t≥0,as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0. We now follow [29] and consider a cylindrical test function, namely, given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} [0,1]$$\end{document}ζ∈[0,1], it is easy to infer, recalling the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _\varepsilon $$\end{document}ωε in (4.7) and the usual Modica–Mortola/Bogomol’nyi trick,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} (t)E_\varepsilon ^S(u_\varepsilon (\cdot ,t))\,\text {d} t\le \int _{0}^\infty \eta (t)E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon (\cdot ,t))\,\text {d} t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫Ω×(0,∞)ζ(x)η(t)dωε(x,t)≤∫0∞η(t)EεS(uε(·,t))dt≤∫0∞η(t)Eε(uε(·,t))dt,recalling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta \in [0,1]$$\end{document}ζ∈[0,1] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^P_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon )\ge 0$$\end{document}EεP(uε)≥0. By means of the compactness (4.19) and dominated convergence first, then by monotone convergence to eliminate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta $$\end{document}ζ, we are led to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{\Omega \times (0,\infty )}\eta (t)\text {d} \omega (x,t)\le \int _0^\infty \eta (t)E(t)\,\text {d} t, $$\end{document}∫Ω×(0,∞)η(t)dω(x,t)≤∫0∞η(t)E(t)dt,which allows to conclude (see the proof of [29, Lemma 2] for the details) that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω can be disintegrated as in the statement of the theorem, and also that (4.39) is satisfied. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ a similar result can be shown, up to another disintegration in the p-variable, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\in \mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}p∈Sd-1. We need now to pass to the limit in the velocity equation. To this aim, as first introduced in [29], we aim at showing that the couple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\psi _\varepsilon ,V_\varepsilon )$$\end{document}(ψε,Vε) satisfies an approximate transport equation of the form4.46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t\psi _\varepsilon +V_\varepsilon \omega _\varepsilon =\partial _t\psi _\varepsilon +V_\varepsilon {\textbf {n}} _\varepsilon \cdot \nabla \psi _\varepsilon \approx 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tψε+Vεωε=∂tψε+Vεnε·∇ψε≈0,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {n}} _\varepsilon :=\frac{\nabla \psi _\varepsilon }{\left| \nabla \psi _\varepsilon \right| }=\frac{\nabla u_\varepsilon }{\left| \nabla u_\varepsilon \right| }$$\end{document}nε:=∇ψε∇ψε=∇uε∇uε is the approximate normal vector which transports the quantity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _\varepsilon $$\end{document}ψε. In particular, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0, we rigorously verify (4.46) and we also obtain that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t\psi \ll \omega $$\end{document}∂tψ≪ω, so that we can define a final normal velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V:=-\frac{\text {d} \partial _t\psi }{\text {d} \omega }$$\end{document}V:=-d∂tψdω, which is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω-measurable and such that, by construction,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _t\psi +V\omega =0, $$\end{document}∂tψ+Vω=0,in the sense of measures, which is a kind of transport equation. The only difference lies in the fact that the information on the support of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω is not enough to guarantee that it is the same as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \nabla \psi \right| $$\end{document}∇ψ, but we have only (see Remark 3.4) \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \nabla \psi \right| \le \omega $$\end{document}∇ψ≤ω in the sense of measures. In conclusion, in the proposition we provide a sharp energy dissipation inequality between the dissipative term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \varepsilon (\partial _t u_\varepsilon )^2$$\end{document}∫ε(∂tuε)2 and the velocity V, which is essential to prove the validity of an energy inequality à la De Giorgi.

Proof

The proof can be carried out following word by word the proofs of Lemma 3 and Proposition 3 of [29]. The essential ingredient is the pointwise nonpositivity of the discrepancy measure, which corresponds to (4.33), together with (4.35)–(4.36). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

In the following last proposition, we give a sharp inequality between the gradient terms in the energy dissipation inequalities. Namely it (formally) gives a sharp lower bound for the term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int \frac{1}{\varepsilon }(\delta E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon ))$$\end{document}∫1ε(δEε(uε)) in terms of the corresponding mean curvature terms in the sharp interface limit. Most of the proof is already present in [32] (see also [29, Proof of Proposition 4]), but here we need to crucially adapt it due to the presence of the Lagrange multipliers. Similar arguments can be found in [13, 47]. We have

Proposition 4.7

Proof

We can finally put together all of these results above to conclude the proof of Theorem 3.5.

Proof of Theorem 3.5

The convergence results come directly from the previous propositions. In particular, convergence (3.18) has been obtained in the proof of Proposition 4.5 (see (4.44)). To check that the limit is a De Giorgi-type varifold solution, we check the items in Definition 3.1. First, the velocity V satisfies (3.3a) by Proposition 4.6, as well as property (3.3b) is verified by Proposition 4.7. Then (3.3c) and (3.3e) are given in Proposition 4.1. Also, the compatibility condition (3.3f) is shown to hold in Lemma 4.5. Concerning the De Giorgi-type inequality (3.3d), we fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}T∈(0,∞) and we aim at passing to the limit in4.61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon (\,\cdot \,,T))+\frac{1}{2} \int _0^T\int _{\Omega } \varepsilon (\partial _tu_\varepsilon (\,\cdot \,,t))^2 \,\text {d} x\,\text {d} t\nonumber \\&\qquad + \frac{1}{2}\int _0^T\int _{\Omega } \frac{1}{\varepsilon }\left| {\textbf {H}} _\varepsilon -\lambda _\varepsilon \sqrt{2W(u_\varepsilon (\cdot ,t))}\frac{\nabla \psi _\varepsilon }{\left| \nabla \psi _\varepsilon \right| } \right| ^2 \,\text {d} x\,\text {d} t\nonumber \\&\quad \le E_\varepsilon (u_\varepsilon ,0), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Eε(uε(·,T))+12∫0T∫Ωε(∂tuε(·,t))2dxdt+12∫0T∫Ω1εHε-λε2W(uε(·,t))∇ψε∇ψε2dxdt≤Eε(uε,0),but this is now immediate, thanks to the bounds (4.39), (4.49), (4.50) for the left-hand side, as well as (4.32) for the right-hand side.

The last property, i.e., the fact that the unoriented varifold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\widehat{\mu }}_t$$\end{document}μ^t, corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _t$$\end{document}μt (identifying antipodal points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm p$$\end{document}±p on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {S}^{d-1}$$\end{document}Sd-1), is integer \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(d-1)$$\end{document}(d-1)-rectifiable for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}t∈(0,∞), is already proven in [47, Theorem 3.12] (see also [48, Theorem 2.2] for the standard Allen–Cahn case).

In conclusion, by using this time (4.4) we can pass to the limit in a similar way by means of (4.39), (4.49), (4.50) for the left-hand side (clearly the same estimates hold for the time interval (s, t), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0\le s<t$$\end{document}0≤s<t, in place of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(0,\infty )$$\end{document}(0,∞)) and using this time (4.40) for the right-hand side. After passing to the limit as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon \downarrow 0$$\end{document}ε↓0, we thus obtain the validity of (3.3d) with (0, T) replaced by (s, t) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0 and almost every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s<t$$\end{document}s<t with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=0$$\end{document}s=0 included. As a consequence, we immediately see that the total mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\mapsto \omega _t(\Omega )$$\end{document}t↦ωt(Ω) is nonincreasing. This concludes the proof of Theorem 3.5. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proof of Lemma 3.9

In this section we show the consistency of our new notion of De Giorgi-type varifold solution. (i)(Classical solutions are weak solutions). Most of the properties of Definition 3.1 can be shown to hold in a straightforward way from the regularity of the classical solution. The only property which might be less trivial is the De Giorgi-type sharp energy inequality (3.3d). All the other properties in Definition 3.1 are easily verified.

(i)(Classical solutions are weak solutions). Most of the properties of Definition 3.1 can be shown to hold in a straightforward way from the regularity of the classical solution. The only property which might be less trivial is the De Giorgi-type sharp energy inequality (3.3d). (ii)(Smooth weak solutions are classical solutions). Let us consider \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mu ,\chi )$$\end{document}(μ,χ) as a De Giorgi-type varifold solution such that there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\in (0,\infty ]$$\end{document}T∈(0,∞] so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi (x,t)=\chi _{\mathscr {A}(t)}(x)$$\end{document}χ(x,t)=χA(t)(x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _t = |\nabla \chi (\cdot ,t)| \otimes \delta _{\frac{\nabla \chi (\cdot ,t)}{|\nabla \chi (\cdot ,t)|}}$$\end{document}μt=|∇χ(·,t)|⊗δ∇χ(·,t)|∇χ(·,t)| for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T]$$\end{document}t∈[0,T] and some smoothly evolving family \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {A}(t))_{t\in [0,T]}$$\end{document}(A(t))t∈[0,T]. All the other properties in Definition 3.1 are easily verified.

We are left with the discussion of the approximated velocity vector field, which is the main novelty of the present paper. We can proceed in the construction of a vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}} ={\textbf {v}} (t)$$\end{document}v=v(t) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}A(t), for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in [0,T_{\text {strong} }]$$\end{document}t∈[0,Tstrong] which will be then extended to construct the final vector B. Differently from [36], here we do not consider the vector as to be the gradient of the solution of a Neumann–Laplace problem, but rather as the solution to an incompressible Stokes problem. This allows to impose a full Dirichlet boundary condition on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {v}} (t)$$\end{document}v(t), so that the final vector will point exactly in the normal direction on the interface, as desired. It is the right time to verify all the properties of the gradient-flow calibration. Properties (3.24), (3.31), (3.33), (3.38), and (3.39) are a straightforward consequence of the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi ,\vartheta ,\lambda ^*$$\end{document}ξ,ϑ,λ∗ above. Analogously, (3.26), (3.27), and (3.28) come from (6.5) and (6.8). Additionally, property (3.29) can be inferred from (6.6), entailing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {div}\, B=0$$\end{document}divB=0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)$$\end{document}I(t) and thus the result by Lipschitz continuity. Analogously, we can derive (3.30). Indeed, let us define the trivial extension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\overline{B}}(x,t):=B(P_{\mathcal {I}(t)}(x),t))$$\end{document}B¯(x,t):=B(PI(t)(x),t)) over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_\delta (t)$$\end{document}Uδ(t). This entails, by the Lipschitz continuity of B, using the uniform control (6.8) and recalling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vert x-P_{\mathcal {I}(t)}(x) \vert =\text {dist} (x,\mathcal {I}(t))$$\end{document}|x-PI(t)(x)|=dist(x,I(t)),\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| (Id-\nabla s_{\mathcal {I}}\otimes \nabla s_{\mathcal {I}})B \right| = \left| (Id-\nabla s_{\mathcal {I}}\otimes \nabla s_{\mathcal {I}})(B-{\overline{B}}) \right| \le C(T_{\text {strong} }, \mathscr {A})\text {dist} (x,\mathcal {I}(t)), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Id-∇sI⊗∇sI)B=(Id-∇sI⊗∇sI)(B-B¯)≤C(Tstrong,A)dist(x,I(t)),for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \mathcal {U}_\delta (t)$$\end{document}x∈Uδ(t), giving (3.30). Property (3.34) can be obtained as in [36]. Indeed, thanks to (6.7), it is immediate to verify that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _t\vartheta +({\overline{B}}\cdot \nabla )\vartheta =0,\quad \forall x\in \mathcal {U}_\delta (t), $$\end{document}∂tϑ+(B¯·∇)ϑ=0,∀x∈Uδ(t),so that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \partial _t\vartheta +(B\cdot \nabla )\vartheta =((B-{\overline{B}})\cdot \nabla )\vartheta ,\quad \forall x\in \mathcal {U}_\delta (t). $$\end{document}∂tϑ+(B·∇)ϑ=((B-B¯)·∇)ϑ,∀x∈Uδ(t).Therefore (3.34) directly comes from the (uniform in time) Lipschitz properties of B and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ, upon noticing that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\nabla \vartheta =0$$\end{document}∇ϑ=0 on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega \setminus \mathcal {U}_\delta $$\end{document}Ω\Uδ. Similarly, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _{t} \left| \xi \right| ^2+({\overline{B}}\cdot \nabla )\vert \xi \vert ^2=0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tξ2+(B¯·∇)|ξ|2=0in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_\delta (t)$$\end{document}Uδ(t), and thus again, arguing exactly as in [36, Proof of Theorem 2.2],\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left| \partial _{t} \left| \xi \right| ^2+(B\cdot \nabla )\vert \xi \vert ^2 \right| =\left| ((B-{\overline{B}})\cdot \nabla )\left| \xi \right| ^2 \right| \le C\vert s_\mathcal {I} \vert ^2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tξ2+(B·∇)|ξ|2=((B-B¯)·∇)ξ2≤C|sI|2,giving (3.35). Furthermore, to obtain (3.35), we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&{\partial _{t} \xi +(B\cdot \nabla )\xi +(\nabla B)^T\xi =\partial _{t} \xi +({\overline{B}}\cdot \nabla )\xi +(\nabla {\overline{B}})^T\xi +((B-{\overline{B}})\cdot \nabla )\xi +(\nabla (B-{\overline{B}}))^T\xi }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tξ+(B·∇)ξ+(∇B)Tξ=∂tξ+(B¯·∇)ξ+(∇B¯)Tξ+((B-B¯)·∇)ξ+(∇(B-B¯))Tξ,and the first term in the right-hand side vanishes in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_\delta (t)$$\end{document}Uδ(t), whereas the second one can be estimated as in [36, Proof of Theorem 2.2], giving in summary\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\partial _{t} \xi +(B\cdot \nabla )\xi +(\nabla B)^T\xi }= f\xi +O({s_{\mathcal {I}}}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tξ+(B·∇)ξ+(∇B)Tξ=fξ+O(sI),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:=\chi _{\mathcal {U}_\delta (t)}\nabla s_\mathcal {I}\cdot (\nabla {\overline{B}})\nabla s_\mathcal {I}\in L^\infty (\Omega \times (0,T_{\text {strong} }))$$\end{document}f:=χUδ(t)∇sI·(∇B¯)∇sI∈L∞(Ω×(0,Tstrong)) thanks to the regularity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}sI and (6.8). This entails (3.35). In conclusion, to obtain identity (3.37) note that, by the construction above, namely by properties (6.7) and (3.31), on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)$$\end{document}I(t) this identity becomes exactly (3.20), which holds true as a direct consequence of the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}A(t) evolves according to a volume-preserving mean curvature flow. Therefore, (3.37) is obtained also on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U}_\delta (t)$$\end{document}Uδ(t) by the (uniform in time) Lipschitz continuity of all functions involved. This concludes the proof of the existence of a gradient-flow calibration in the sense of Definition 3.10.

Weak–strong uniqueness: Proof of Theorem 3.12

In this section, we aim at proving the weak–strong uniqueness result of Theorem 3.12. To this aim, we will resort to the new gradient-flow calibration (see Theorem 3.11) to approximate the smooth volume-preserving mean curvature flow. Let then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\text {strong} }\in (0,\infty )$$\end{document}Tstrong∈(0,∞) be a finite time horizon, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathscr {A}(t))_{t\in [0,T_{strong}]}$$\end{document}(A(t))t∈[0,Tstrong] be a smoothly evolving family of bounded domains in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω whose associated family of interfaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathcal {I}(t))_{t\in [0,T_{strong}]}$$\end{document}(I(t))t∈[0,Tstrong], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)=\partial \mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}I(t)=∂A(t) is of class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (0,1)$$\end{document}α∈(0,1), for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} Definition 3.8. As already discussed in the introduction and in Sect. 3, the calibration proposed in [36] as well as the one introduced in [34] are not completely satisfying, in the sense that they construct an approximated velocity vector field B which is not pointing in the normal direction on the interface \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(t)$$\end{document}I(t). This is intrinsically assumed in [34], since B admits by construction a tangential vector field on the interface, whereas in [36], where the main property of B is to be divergence free in the domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}A(t), it is just a consequence of the choice of the construction of B in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}A(t). We have shown in Theorem 3.11 that changing this construction allows to retrieve the desired property while preserving the divergence-free condition of B inside \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}(t)$$\end{document}A(t). With this new gradient-flow calibration for the strong solution at hand, we can build up some measures to quantify the distance between a De Giorgi-type varifold solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\chi _{A(t)},\mu )$$\end{document}(χA(t),μ) according to Definition 3.1 (where as usual we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\textbf {n}} :=\frac{\nabla \chi _{A(t)}}{\left| \nabla \chi _{A(t)} \right| }$$\end{document}n:=∇χA(t)∇χA(t) as to be the inward measure-theoretic normal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^*A(t)$$\end{document}∂∗A(t) for almost any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t>0$$\end{document}t>0), and a strong solution for the volume-preserving mean curvature flow. We can finally proceed with the proof of Theorem 3.12, which takes inspiration from [29], but incorporates the difficulties related to the presence of the Lagrange multiplier. In this sense, we are peforming the lift of the weak–strong uniqueness result in [36] to the varifold setting.
